My predecessor, Dr. Sickl, has already said, in my opinion, that the entire right of invalidity in Austria is unfair. You yourself, Mrs Haidlmayr, know that, according to our law, it is dependent on how much you deserve at the time of the accident and the disability and can therefore quite happen that for the same damage suffered due to an accident at work, with low incomes you receive a small payment for life and with high incomes you receive a high payment for life. I believe that the entire right of invalidity should be redesigned in Austria and that the compensation and pain benefits paid for life should be based on the degree of agility and should not be dependent on income. I have therefore set up a working group to discuss these measures now. The disabled organisations of Austria are invited to cooperate in this working group in order to improve the entire right of invalidity in Austria, both human and fairer. One thing I can do is to those people who are after the 1st century. In July 2001, an accident or an accident at work, which will lead to the future: an improvement in the entire right of invalidity in Austria.

[[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] Otherwise, it would be quite contrary to the fact that the government is here concerned with a matter which is exclusively competence of the National Council and, in my opinion, should also be and remain. [[Abg. Ing. Westenthaler: A self-faller again!]] I can now come to the issue of the first reading of the Treaty of Nice. We know that in December the Treaty of Nice was regulated politically. We had a substantial first debate at the beginning of this year. All parliamentarians, all political groups, were well involved in the provisions, in the discussions of this process. You also got the texts. We will probably be one of the first countries of the European Union to present this Treaty of Nice to the National Council for ratification, and I think it is a good thing because this Treaty of Nice is an absolute progress. [[Abg. Dr. Lichtenberger: We did not get a statement from the government!]] We have implemented a number of points in Austria, which are very important to us, and we have achieved many things. The most important thing from our point of view: These Nice decisions make it possible to extend the European Union, a project of historical importance to us, institutionally possible. Secondly – and this is very, very important for us –: We have already discussed issues in the enlargement context in Nice, and this is already going very well, which we are already successfully starting to implement. This is, for example, the question of transitional periods in the free movement of workers. We are in the middle of the discussion on safeguard clauses and safeguards in the area of freedom to provide services. I therefore believe that this enlargement position and the strategy we have adopted are absolutely meaningful here. [[Unrest in the hall.]] [[President Dr. Fischer gives the bell sign.]] We have proved that one can think Europeanally and at the same time represent national interests and should by no means disregard them. We have important concerns in this Treaty of Nice as regards environmental policy, unanimity, transport, we have been able to lay down in the Treaty the general principle of environmental matters. Our water rights have remained secure. We have made significant progress in the area of asylum and foreign law. Overall, this is a very reasonable solution from the Austrian point of view. We have achieved a very good compromise on the institutional issues, which have, of course, been the most critical. First point: We have achieved that every decision of the Union in the future should be borne by the majority of the states – if you have the kindness to do so elsewhere – and I have my documents – as well as by the majority of the citizens. [[Abg. Dr. Pilz distributed in the hall documents and also places them on the square of the Federal Chancellor]] This is, in any case, a fundamental principle of democracy, and it is also to be lived in the European Union. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] You are familiar with the first proposals of the French Presidency: eight votes for Austria and one board of directors in the Commission. [[Abg. May. Schweitzer: (in the document still distributed). Dr. Pilz sage) The mushroom could be used as a sergeant!]] We have reached nine and at the end even ten votes, with a population of 1.5 percent and a budget share of 2.6% having a vote weight of more than 4 percent for today's fifteen. I think we can live very well with this result. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] We have the principle: Every country must be represented in the institutions, as well as it was somehow, actually secured, and that will be so for a very long time in the field of the Commission. ==References====External links== Member State is required to adopt a new, but also a unanimous, decision. So we have resisted the Executive Board of the Great, just as Luxembourg Prime Minister Juncker said publicly these days before Parliament or the Belgian Prime Minister Verhofstadt a few weeks ago. I stand by it because Europe must be built on the balance of large and small, southern, central and northern states, west and east, and this must be done at the level of equality and balance! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Of course, there is still a bit of an interest in Nice, because for the first time now we have a legal procedure on the issues that hit us in a massive way last year, namely the sanctions against Austria, and how important this is, we now see in the Italian debate. No one today comes to the idea of arbitrarily imposing sanctions on an important member country. [[Abg. Dr. Lichtenberger: It's too late for Bossi!]] Everyone now – rightly, I say – appeals to those procedures that we have, in an interesting way, proposed – or surprisingly – with the Belgians and which are then dealt with in a legal manner with checks up to the European Court of Justice, with the referral of Parliaments, the National Parliaments and the European Parliament. I think this is good, and it is very, very important so that there can never be anything like it again, what happened in the case of Austria. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Furthermore, Nice was the starting point for a discussion: the future of Europe. First of all, the procedure – and I say this very openly here; it is for the first time that I say this formally as the head of government –: I think we should choose an open system. In the first phase, after the mandate has been defined – which will be the case in Laaken under the Belgian Presidency – an open model of the Convention should be made, where possible, if possible, representatives of governments, national parliaments, the European Parliament, but also scientists, lawyers, international lawyers, should be involved. Such a convention model has the huge advantage that it can actually come to a broad public referral. I do not want the discussion to take place, the important discussion about the future of Europe behind closed doors. There is a need for a broad democratic participation here. That will be the Austrian position, ladies and gentlemen. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] At the same time, however, this Convention has to work differently than in the Charter of Fundamental Rights, because one will not go: that you do almost a "Take it or leave it": A text is prepared and can and must not be changed. In my opinion, that was also the beauty mistake at the Convention, at the Charter. We Austrians had many wishes, for example in minority law, in the language regime. So it can't be that the representatives put something on our table, which can't be changed under any circumstances. That is why – and I hope that this will not only be 2004, but I hope it will be already in the course of 2003 – an official Intergovernmental Conference, which, of course, also needs to be recoupled with the national parliaments, will take decisions and additions that are absolutely necessary for this debate. So much for the procedure. I hope you can follow me together in this way. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] On the question of competence – second important topic. – We agreed in Nice that a catalogue of competence should now be developed: What is Europe supposed to do, what are the national states to do, and what is to happen at the subsidiary level – with us these are the countries or communities? In my opinion, this discussion is quite, very important. Don't forget that someone like Wolfgang Schäuble, who is really unwarranted, has clearly said: Without clarifying the question of competence, the principle of subsidiarity cannot be implemented, and this must be contained in the European Constitution. – This question of how we regulate the reorganisation of competences will be crucial, and that must be clarified. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] Ladies and gentlemen! It must not be so that any discussion is immediately labeled "European hostile". That would be a fatal mistake, and I quote a very different, also unwarranted, at least equally unwarranted witness, the North Rhine-Westphalian Prime Minister Wolfgang Clement, who in a very interesting article and presentation in February of this year exactly referred to this competence issue and de facto proposed three patterns of competence, clusters: an exclusive European competence, which only the EU has, in particular, foreign, foreign and monetary policy, also common foreign and security policy, and other areas which include a principle of legislation with very much scope for the nation states, and other areas which retain only a complementary competence where the national states – or the countries and communities in our country – are primarily required. I believe that such a discussion, a clarification, what subsidiarity, which means task correction, will be absolutely important. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and the Freeman.]] Ladies and gentlemen! In this context, however, I would also like to mention some of the proposals that have been made recently. It has about the German SPD Proposals made in a lead proposal: Responsibility for Europe, which is, of course, now being discussed throughout Europe. I think that such a proposal is absolutely useful. Other parties, such as us in Austria, have also made similar proposals or other suggestions. Two points are of great interest, and I would like to draw attention to them, because they will be very important in the future. The first point concerns the proposal, which will clearly be European in any case, namely the question of the common foreign, security and defence policy. It is clear from this document that in this area – literally – "in the medium term a community of this policy area", namely foreign, security and defence policy, "is sought". – This is an interesting question: a possible union of foreign, security and defence policy! [[Abg. Kiss:... Costelka...! Only he doesn't want to do it! And neither is the arbitrator!]] This is the case in relation to: Area also: "The transatlantic partnership remains the basis of our security in Europe. All the way!]] It would be very interesting to hear whether this position of the SPD is also shared by the Austrian Social Democrats, because more Europe will really mean a union of the policy approach also in the field of foreign, security and defence policy. I'm for that! But it would be wise if we could perhaps bring about an Austrian consensus on this, and it would be good if such a thing were possible, ladies and gentlemen. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] The second point, which has been proposed and which is of course interesting and controversial, concerns the question of the renationalisation of certain policy areas. This SPD document states that some areas – literally – must be "relocated to the national level": "This applies particularly to the EU's competence in the fields of agricultural and structural policy," – quote. In my opinion, this question should be discussed in the sense of sine ira et studio, which is very reasonable. But at the same time you have to put your finger on the point: What does this mean? What does it mean to relocate 80 percent, four-fifths of the European Union's current budget to national competence for 80 percent of the European Union's tasks? Ladies and gentlemen! That means simply and simply not "more Europe", but that means 80 percent "less Europe" – to say it quite clearly. [[Abg. May. Schweitzer: This bill is not true! That's not it!]] Secondly, it means, of course, that this also changes some of the political content, and in some areas it means the end of the common market. I have to say: If I have a market order and pay premiums for it, there are no distortions of competition in this area. However, renationalisation means that there will again be national competition opportunities. The result will be exactly the competition war, the promotion war that has already occurred before the creation of the internal market in these areas. But that helps the great and harms the little ones. You have to say that openly! Ladies and gentlemen! It also weakens the external appearance of the European Union. Of course, in the case of the World Trade Organisation, the EU can become more credible if it really does carry out environmental standards and other issues in the agricultural sector in common, without distortions and differentiations. In addition, it is almost ridiculous, almost absurd, to force the candidate countries to make some major sacrifices and make structural reforms, such as Polish agriculture, but at the same time to think about a renationalisation of agricultural and support policies. [[Abg. Dr. Lichtenberger: There has been this debate for five years!]] That this, of course, also means de-olidarization – especially for a large net payer; We don't need to mention names here – it's clear, but it's an effect that you should say. Ladies and gentlemen! However, what would be useful – and I would like to have this discussion in Austria – is the question of whether we should not re-transform, also more in agricultural policy, from the first to the so-called second pillar, from the market order in rural areas, to environmental policy, for which our farmers – and for which we really thank them – do more than any other European country has done so far! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] If we are in the second pillar – which is co-financed by the way: in the southern countries a little less, but with us up to 50 percent! – would be transformed, of course, would result in greater involvement by the nations, the Member States, and would have achieved a significant environmental policy steering effect. Ladies and gentlemen! That would be interesting! If we were to increase this share of 10 per cent today to 20, 30 per cent – which corresponds to so many ideas in the Commission that I like well – it would not be all in the south of Europe, but would be an interesting solution, which would also be in the interest of the net payers and in the interest of the rural family farms and the environment. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP and on representatives of the Free Trade Unions.]] In such a reform of the common agricultural policy, it is also possible to reintroduce our subject - which we repeatedly demand - into the ceiling on support, degressively, so that the small ones are encouraged more strongly than the big ones. It is even possible to consider whether or not a mandatory co-financing should be ensured in the market area – but please, without distortion of competition and on a clear European basis! These are discussions and these are topics that I believe would make sense. Ladies and gentlemen! Thirdly and last point: I believe that we should not be able to understand Europe so much from the institutions and from the games of power and power, but rather that we would be more important to the citizens to explain why some of the things we are currently discussing within the framework of the European Agenda are so important. Enlargement, for example, if we also implement our environmental standards in the candidate countries, will reduce the ozone burden in Austria by 40 percent. There will be a 60 percent reduction in sulphur dioxide emissions as soon as – and if so – the candidate countries take on the standards that are of course ours. Enlargement and thus the expansion of the Schengen area, a common European border police force, for example, which jointly and effectively protects the Union's external borders, will reduce the burden and threat potential of tractors, smuggling, drug traffickers and everything else in this area. This is in Austria's interest, for in 1998 we had, for example, 20,000 seizures of illegals, already 46 000 last year. These are the topics that interest citizens: Environment, security, standard of living, common gains in peace and stability! These issues should come to the fore, and not so much the question: Who wins, who loses institutionally anything? I also say openly: Many European statesmen have already spoken out. [[President Dr. Fischer gives the bell sign]] I quote two of them. Giuliano Amato said: More Europe means sometimes less Europe! – This was very targeted at the competence and subsidiarity discussion. Tony Blair meant: We have to develop Europe into a superpower, not into a superstate!

Democracy is part of the public legislation, democracy is a clear definition of what is responsible, what Europe and what is at national level for, and democracy belongs to the fact that citizens also have the impression – right! – that they can influence what is happening in Europe with their democratic electoral decisions. However, I am firmly convinced that, as in the past, a further enlargement in the agricultural policy will not be able to restore consumer confidence in Europe. We need a fresh start in Europe's agricultural policy, and Austria should also make its contribution to that. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! If we want the basic interests and needs of the citizens, the workers of Europe, to have a service society equipped with a social conscience and social solidarity, then the integration of the European Union cannot remain at the point achieved so far. What has happened in the economic field, in the political field, must be continued at the social and environmental level. Therefore, a Europe that serves its people will only be a Europe which is also a socially and ecologically integrated Europe, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen. ParlaMint-AT_2001-05-11-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00070_d7e6648	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! Some have already mentioned that for the first time in this summit, since Austria has been a member of the European Union since many years, it has really been tried to reach a broad consensus in Austria on the measures that Austria called for in Nice, with regard to the policy represented by the official Austria in Nice, and to achieve by permanent coordination with the opposition that both the ratification can be carried out in great agreement and the further steps of Austria can be designed in the way. I tell you personally: I would have liked the process before Austria's accession to the European Union could have taken place in such a broad consensus and with such intensive information and also involvement of the opposition – with critical voices being heard. We might have been able to prevent so many things that we have had to discuss in recent years through such a broad discussion before accession to the European Union. [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] Institutionally, I believe, it is particularly important for us that it has succeeded in ensuring equal representation of all EU Member States in the institutions of the European Union. It is also important for us to ensure that the weighting of votes could prevent the massive overweight of large EU Member States – which some countries have intended to do – and that the principle of unanimity – and that is, I believe, particularly important for a small country like Austria. It could be enforced that the principle of unanimity continues to apply to important issues that concern us. [[Congratulations with the liberals and the ÖVP.]] There are very many of these important questions. In the future, for example, no decision should be taken over the heads of the Austrians over our water resources. The same applies to the issue of soil management and the issue of immigration and asylum. So this is a very important result that Austria was able to achieve! With regard to EU enlargement, we should be positive in that, in the area of free movement of workers, the transitional periods of up to seven years could be set. I do not know why this is such a problem for you, Mrs Lunacek. It says that these are transitional periods of up to seven years. I believe that this is in the interest not only of the Member States of the European Union, but also of the candidate countries. It cannot be in our interest, on the one hand, to bring cheap workers into the EU, but on the other hand, it cannot be in our interest to remove highly qualified workers from these countries who need to build up their structures, to improve their economic structures. I therefore believe that this is a reasonable measure for both sides, both for the candidate countries and for the Member States of the European Union, and especially for Austria. It is important that we signal that enlargement of the European Union is an important project, an important goal for Europe, but also for Austria, that we signal that Europe is more than the European Union. But this project of a common, united Europe must not take place on the back of the population. It must be a chance and must not be a danger. [[Congratulations with the liberals and the ÖVP.]] It is therefore important that these criteria be established, but also met, not only the criteria for free movement in the labour market, but also the criteria for environmental standards – keyword: Temelin – and the criteria for the constitutional standards – keyword: Beneš decrees and AVNOJ provisions. All these are important areas with which we should signal: Europe is more than a combination of economies and business economies, but Europe becomes a community of values. These values must, of course, also be shown in the candidate countries, but of course also in the Member States of the European Union. This is a clear objective, and everyone who meets these criteria is welcome in this union of democratic states. This should be clearly and consistently represented at the international level, I believe, in the country, but also at the international level. Because you have put the claim in the room, we would only represent Austrian interests: I believe, of course, that on the one hand, we should support the objective of a united Europe in an important process, but, of course, we are committed to the Austrians. We have the mandate of the Austrian population and, of course, the responsibility to represent the interests of Austria and Austria vis-à-vis the EU institutions and the international organisations. [[Congratulations with the liberals and the ÖVP.]] Ladies and gentlemen! A third area, which has been somewhat undervalued in the previous debate, is the question of a united Europe also by means of a common security and defence policy. I believe that this question is wrongfully put in the context of economic cooperation, labour and environmental issues. There will be no common economic policy in the long term, there will be no common environmental policy, there will be no common social policy if Europe fails to build a common peace and security system on the continent. It can be quite simply – especially when we see ourselves as a community of values – no longer permitted in the future and no longer uninfluenced that wars are being waged on our continent, that are being murdered, raped on our continent, without the democratic community giving a clear signal to end these crises and these human rights violations. In this respect, we have had to undergo a very painful learning process in the last ten years, and we have needed long enough to draw lessons from the crisis in the Balkans. Thank God – and I say this with great optimism – the European Union is now also on its way to developing common security structures – not against someone, but for values, for security, for freedom, for human rights in Europe. This should be positively assessed by all of us and supported with all our strength! [[Congratulations with the liberals and representatives of the ÖVP.]] This is a project in which not only the 15 EU Member States are to be involved, but also all the candidate countries, as well as all other European countries – many have also made concrete contributions to European crisis management – are invited to participate in this so important security project. We will have to be credible in this respect in the foreseeable future, and we will also have to present in relevant contributions that there will be, yes, a single political will in Europe in the future. I believe that the question of common security policy and, within this framework, the question of common defence can lead to a much stronger integration of Europe and also to a stronger training of a Europe-consciousness. This should also be important to us as the responsible politicians, namely that Europe should be formed not only in the minds of politicians and economic professionals in the future, but also in the hearts of people on this continent. It is necessary to signal that we represent common values. For this to happen, it is also necessary to provide comprehensive information for the population on the advantages and disadvantages of European integration. But it is also necessary that we, the politicians acting, take seriously the concerns and fears of the people, accept them and do everything we can to dispel these concerns. Specific measures will make it possible to make the project of a common Europe a future opportunity for future generations. I can only hope that this national consensus, which has now been mentioned in many debates, will exist in all areas of European integration policy – always in the light of the fact that we also have a mandate from the Austrians to represent the interests of this country. It is important that we represent a single line in social policy, labour market policy, economic policy and security policy, so that we can create an order on our continent for future generations in terms of a common economy, common environmental standards, but also in terms of common security and peace enforcement. Today, I believe, we have reached an important point in European history and thus also in Austrian history. It is in our hands whether we can create this project of a united, populous Europe for future generations. The cooperation of all is also required in the future processes, such as in the so-called post-nizza process. The question of democratisation of the European Union will be important. The question of information processes will also be important. Last but not least, fraud will also be important to enforce the principle of subsidiarity in concrete terms. There can be no taboo for regulatory mechanisms to regulate different materials, but this should be done solely according to the criteria of reason and effectiveness and should not be geared towards institutional and bureaucratic needs. I believe that we are on a good path here, that we have to represent the people accordingly, but that we must not lose sight of the vision of this important project of a united, but above all of a peaceful Europe.

Let me also say a few words about the economic prospects of this integration process, after the previous speakers spoke primarily about foreign policy and security aspects of the Treaty of Nice. The Treaty of Nice has set very important ways of ensuring that the European Union is able to develop and open up. If we look at the current trend from an economic point of view, we must say that Austria's previous membership of the European Union has moved economically to where we have always listened historically and geographically, namely to the heart of Europe. This is also extremely important for Austria's economic location. Austria is on the one hand a member of the European Union, but on the other hand through traditional Eastern trade know-how, through traditional Eastern trade contacts for many international companies, a very suitable location. We are therefore able to fulfil a bridge function to Eastern Europe, to those countries which are at the door of the European Union. We have also benefited economically from membership of the European Union. The dynamics of the internal market has led to the increase in exports to the European Union from 380 billion to 580 billion schilling in the six years of our membership – this is a plus of 200 billion schilling! Conversion to jobs means that 150 000 to 200 000 jobs were secured by membership and by the dynamics of the internal market in Europe. That also means: Full employment policy through the combination of national measures and the dynamics of the internal market. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] This membership has also made economic but also caused wide areas of our economic law to be ventilated as well and fresh wind has come in. The whole liberalisation in the telecommunications sector and in the energy sector was a fresh wind, which ultimately came from Europe. We are very happy about this because it has also improved Austria's economic location. For years, many international companies have meant: Your business location has two major disadvantages: You are far too expensive in the field of energy, and you are far too expensive in the field of telecommunications. – The liberalisation that this Federal Government has done has brought about these two disadvantages. We are also competitive again in these areas, in terms of energy and telecoms costs, in Europe, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen. [[Congratulation with the ÖVP and the Abg. Böhacker.]] Since the introduction of the euro – as of the coming January, the citizens will experience this very clearly – we have the next great advantage, namely that the fluctuations in the currency have been removed within the area of the members of the euro area. This too is an incredible location advantage for our economy. Finally, I think you have to say: Economic location ultimately means full employment for Austria and income opportunities for the employed. Finally, this is also the basis of social security which we have in Austria, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen. This is also a success of EU membership and the policy of this federal government. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] Ladies and gentlemen! If we now see the whole thing in the light of the forthcoming enlargement of the European Union, let me say one thing very clearly. If we are honest, we must admit that Austria, out of the eastern opening alone, was actually the great winner economically. I have recently read a study of the Spatial Planning Conference, which states, for example, that between 1989 and 1999, in these ten years, of the 25 political districts of Austria, which are located at the EU's external border – i.e. the border with the candidate countries – 21 regions had higher economic growth and higher employment growth within this period than was recorded in the total Austrian average. This also shows the chances of the regions as far as the opening of the East is concerned, ladies and gentlemen. Let me once again address the export industry. While our exports have doubled worldwide in the last ten years, they have quadrupled in Eastern Europe. Today, we have reached a state that around 48 billion Schilling exports market in Hungary are as important as the entire US export market, which is also 48 billion Schilling. We have experienced that the small Slovenia as an export market today, in terms of quantity and value, is 40 percent more important than Japan. It thus shows an incredible dynamic also of these countries, which are now at the door of the European Union. We have truly unique opportunities when we see this economically when we see it in terms of growth opportunities, income opportunities and jobs, ladies and gentlemen. Of course, we also have another great opportunity – I say this above all as one that comes from the economy. At the beginning, many of our businesses, particularly in the border area – and I am the representative of a border region – complained that there is an unfair competition there, that products from low-cost countries and countries with little environmental costs come from Austria as well and that equal opportunities do not exist in competition. What does it mean, though, when these countries become members of the European Union? – It means that equal opportunities will again exist, because these candidate countries must raise their level, for example, in the area of labour law, social law or environmental law to our level. Equal opportunities are therefore an essential element in ensuring that our businesses also have more competitive opportunities in the border areas compared to what is happening today. I think it is often overlooked that, when these countries are lifted at European level in terms of environmental provisions and labour law, we will have these equal opportunities again. Ladies and gentlemen! But let me also say: We still have a number of problems to solve and take a number of necessary measures. A number of previous speakers have rightly addressed the issue of labour market and transitional periods. Mr President of the Council! I understand the concerns of workers' representatives – no question at all – but I believe that we must see this offensively. I would remind you that we had the debate in the main committee on whether, after enlargement, the Commissioners would be able to move around, whether Austria would be able to issue another Commissioner. In Nice it has been decided that up to 27 members of the European Union will be able to appoint each country a Commissioner. If there should be more members beyond this 27, there will have to be a decision on this again. However, the larger countries, which are now two Commissioners, will only be able to appoint one Commissioner from 2005 onwards. On the weighting of votes in the Council. I can still remember very well when the main committee asked whether we would still give our assent to a reduction of four to three or two. The weighting of votes in the Council by ten votes has remained close to the same ratio as it has been before, for Germany as the largest country, which has ten times as many inhabitants as Austria, has 29 votes in the Council. One important point for us was the unanimity on the issues of spatial planning, on the issues of water resources, as well as on the issues of land use and energy sources, and on the free choice of energy supply. Austria can also hold twelve members in the Economic and Social Committee. The largest countries in the European Union will be able to make 24 members in the future. This is where our countries are represented accordingly. The number of Austrian mandates in the European Parliament has been slightly reduced. In the future, only more than 17 mandates can be elected from Austria, but only about half of the votes required for a German mandate in the European Parliament are necessary for an Austrian mandate in the European Parliament. For the future of Europe, we do not need the unbridled verbal rapid shots of a Joschka fisherman, nor the fleeting sayings of a Gerhard Schröder, who – and certainly will not be able to dispute this too – could not prevail against the French in Nice. Our image of a united Europe is that of the founding fathers of the European Union – a Konrad Adenauer, a Robert Schuman or also a Charles de Gaulle – a Europe of the father countries and not a single union of the European states, possibly in the form of a centralist state. [[Congratulations with the liberals and representatives of the ÖVP.]] From the Austrian point of view, one can be quite satisfied with the outcome of Nice, because the German Government has effectively defended the Austrian interests, in particular the areas sensitive to Austria and has also enforced Parliament's negotiating mandate. The extension of majority decisions has taken place in those areas where this was useful; However, majority decisions did not become the rule. In the sensitive areas of immigration policy, asylum and refugee policy, as well as water management, spatial planning and land use, as well as in essential transport issues, unanimity was maintained as a principle. In this context, it should be noted that social democracy and also the Greens were responsible for the task of the principle of unanimity in asylum policy and the Greens in water management. The principle of unanimity will continue to be maintained in the case of legislative acts with constitutional nature, but also in the case of acts requiring national ratification. I have already mentioned the water industry: Austria is no longer able to agree in this area, which is now more clearly regulated than in the old Treaty of Amsterdam. The principle of unanimity applies also in the areas of land use, spatial planning, the choice of energy sources, but also in terms of the principles of transport regulations, i.e. basic transport policy decisions. And what is particularly important to us in the area of border, the principles of asylum, immigration and refugee policy remain the subject of unanimity. In other words, the Federal Government has been able to ensure, on all sensitive issues, that it cannot be decided over the heads of the Austrians. The abolition of veto rights, which some have represented, has not taken place in Nice. As far as voting weighting is concerned, it should be noted that there has been only a significant appreciation of the large Member States as a compensation for the abandonment of a second Commissioner. Decisions shall be taken by a minimum of 258 of the total of 345 votes. With regard to the issue of block-minority, there is – and this is also very gratifying – no barrier minority for three large Member States. If all candidates will have joined, then the blocking minority will rise from 88 to 91 votes. In addition, it was ensured that a qualified majority always includes the majority of Member States. Furthermore, a member of the Council may, when deciding whether this qualified majority covers at least 62 percent of the total population of the Union. If this is not fulfilled, the decision in question shall not be taken. But of fundamental importance to us Austrians was also the enforcement of the rule of law in the famous Article 7 procedure. In this respect, the Austrian ideas have been fully realized. As some previous speakers have said, there can be no more arbitrary sanctions, as we have seen them in the past, against our country. There can no longer be short-term action to this extent, as we had to take note of it, because the European Parliament must agree. Furthermore, the legal hearing of the person concerned is ensured at all stages of the proceedings and the obligation to state reasons is provided for in such a step. In addition, it is established that decisions will also have to be appropriate in the future. Verification by the European Court of Justice at the request of the Member State concerned on compliance with the rules of procedure has also been established. Of course, for us Austrians – I have already noted this – the border regions have a very special importance, especially in the case of enlargement of the European Union. The opportunities, but also the risks, are, of course, particularly great for the border regions. It is therefore a particular concern for us to support border regions optimally in averting the risk factors. At the Berlin European Council, 5 billion Schilling – of course with co-financing – were negotiated for the border regions. On the Austrian proposal, the European Commission was asked at the Nice European Council to propose a programme to strengthen the economic competitiveness of the border regions, and it will be very important for us to be very intensive in preparing these proposals. I'll finish. The main speaker of Social Democracy, Mr Gusenbauer, spoke of a provocation and irritation strategy of the Austrian Federal Government. I tried to hear what he meant by that, but that's not so easy at all. It is alleged that the concept of the strategic partnership with him and, according to his statements, with the neighbouring countries, creates confusion. It is of course the right of honourable Member Gusenbauer to be the leader in opposition to the government, but to speak of a provocation and irritation strategy in Austria is simply wrong, excessive and ultimately misleading. [[Inter-calls at the SPÖ.]] What is the purpose of this provocation and this irritation? What does Gusenbauer mean – which is not in the hall even now – actually? Should Austria refrain from exercising its legitimate interests and be based solely on the wishes of the candidate countries? - I mean, it can't be. We, the government parties, ladies and gentlemen, understand us primarily as Austrian lawyers and not as advocates of foreign interests! Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2001-05-11-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00070_d7e8619	Mr President. Federal Chancellor! Minister! High house! This Treaty of Nice certainly leaves an ambivalent impression, and nothing could make this clearer than that essential questions about the future of the European Union were delegated to the so-called post-nizza process. Although some conditions have been created for the formal and institutional development of the EU, for example on the issue of voting weighting. However, much that could lead to a deepening of the integration process remains open. Thus, decisive steps have not been taken to strengthen the democratic principle in the EU. Essentially, in Nice, the Council has become increasingly strong within the EU institutions, apart from smaller shifts of competence in favour of the President of the Commission. It has been shown that national interests remain a priority in the substantive issues of common European decisions, such as tax policy or social policy. In essence, by re-weighting the votes in the Council, by binding to the population keys, the great nations have also prevailed against the smaller ones. In future, Austria will have 10 votes in the Council, compared with 29 for France, instead of 4 to 10. The main objective of the Austrian Federal Government to keep an Austrian Commissioner on a permanent basis could also not be achieved. [[Abg. Dr. Spindelegger: such a fool!]] Many issues relating to the deepening of political integration remain reserved for the post-Nizza process, for example the question of a transparent reform process in the course of a Constitutional Convention, the question of strengthening the European Parliament, such as the transfer of budgetary sovereignty, the future position of the Commission as a European government, the direct election of the President of the Commission and much more. However, many of the problems of acceptance of the European Union in the population will not be able to be solved until the EU succeeds in this post-Nizza process to take greater account of the interests of workers, as will those of consumers, as has been shown in the BSE scandal, for example, to what extent interest representatives, parties, trade unions, NGOs at European level will be heard. For the future development of Europe, it will be crucial to see to what extent the citizens of Europe understand this Union as a place of political participation, in which not only the economic conditions for a decent existence are given, but also their social, political, cultural and fundamental needs are met. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] In this context, the process and debate related to the EU Charter of Fundamental Rights could pave the way. Those representatives of the Heads of State or Government, MEPs, national parliamentarians and Commission representatives, who formulated a Charter of Fundamental Rights in the previous year, were faced with the most difficult task of finding a balance between the stronger integration of social fundamental rights on the one hand and their legal commitment on the other, and thus their democratic legitimacy on the other. We have not only been satisfied with traditional rights of freedom and participation, but we have also formulated provisions to protect individuals from economic and social exploitation. In future, the Charter will be an instrument for defending people when their rights of work, health, social security and education are cut or threatened. Therefore, the proclamation of the Charter in Nice is a first step. Of course, the implementation into the Treaties, and thus the achievement of legal obligations, must only follow before such a European Constitution can be formulated in a further step. The Charter certainly also suffers from many contradictions arising from the different nature of integration in the Member States. Much remains open or is dissatisfied, such as rewording the right to work into a right. Another point is the lack of the right to a minimum income and thus a decent existence – that is not the same as the right to social support, as stated in Article 34 of the Charter. Every person, without regard to gender or age, has the right to a guaranteed individual income, the right to a minimum income, the right to a minimum pension and a minimum wage. [[Conjugation with the SPÖ and the Abg. Oils.]] The European Union therefore needs, in particular, a social agenda worthy of this name, particularly with regard to the enlargement of the Union, if one wants to take account not only of the interests of investors, merchants and traders, but also of the interests of the working people and their living conditions. Therefore, there is still a wide-ranging discussion on the development of the European Union in order to increase the acceptance of the population and to define the Austrian position, which is currently not recognizable at best – and to which I would like to call on the government to do so. It takes time to do this. It makes no sense to speed up the enlargement of the Union hypertrophic and cynically endanger the social security of people. Therefore, the ratification of the Treaty of Nice also has time, at least until the positioning of the only democratic sovereign of the EU that was not involved in Nice, namely the European Parliament in the second half of 2001. "Speed kills" – especially people. In the meantime, you should know that from your own point of view, and therefore you are asked to get into the process of ratification particularly carefully.

In legal terms, we still meet the needs, and the Federal Minister is now trying to close the legal gaps. The current papers are part of this work. The essential rules have already been addressed by Mr Murauer and Mr Gaál, so I do not need to address them too much. One important point I would like to mention is the harmonisation of disciplinary law, which has become necessary in this form for foreign operations, because in the past there have often been very difficult legal situations, which have been solved with a pseudosolution of repatriation, i.e. with the home sending of the person concerned, which is not satisfactory in the long term. Finally, let me break a lance for foreign operations and in favour of these soldiers in principle. I have already said that foreign use is becoming more and more a normal case for our federal army. He's interested in all of us. It is in our interest to maintain or restore peace in advance in order to at least limit the impact on Austria. In principle, these interventions find the approval of all parties, even those of the Greens – if I remember correctly, they were the last time they opposed the withdrawal of Cyprus's use. This sounds nice, the politicians of all governments praise these efforts again and again and they like to be filmed at the places of use with the soldiers. However, we should also take the consequences from these interventions. If, for example, brigade-strong forces are constantly abroad, in demanding and increasingly demanding and dangerous applications, then it costs something. For example, a truck that is used in border use or perhaps even on bad roads in Bosnia, as it was, carry out non-stop transports, hundreds, if not thousands of journeys with materials to rebuild houses, schools and so on, is much earlier than a truck that is used in Austria for training in the German army. And if, for example, the German army demands that it support the construction in Bosnia, then it should be given the necessary resources, ladies and gentlemen, too. – This is something we should really consider. Former governments have given additional orders to the federal army, but have retained financial resources for it – that was not serious. Minister Scheibner is criticized if he takes the consequences and takes heavy weight formations – that is the only means that he currently has no more money – and, for example, reduces the less important use of Cyprus. It is unfair to the federal army to argue in this way. To conclude: Our soldiers, especially those abroad, who fulfill a demanding and often dangerous task and serve our country, have a right – a right! – to the best possible equipment and the best possible protection. It's fucking unfair to charge such expenses with expensive highway pickerln or ambulance fees, as Mrs. Lichtenberger recently did on television in the "ZiB 3". Those who argue in this way, ladies and gentlemen of the Greens, have lost every claim to seriality!

It has also said that it is important that a public debate has now begun in Turkey, but that the form, as it should be, is not yet well known and that it takes time. The Minister also wrote: "There are no compromises in respect of human rights." Ladies and gentlemen! This is quite a contradiction, because if there are no compromises, then it is not possible to say that one hopes that Turkey will present something now, one will discuss it in one's own meeting behind closed doors, one must not put pressure on Turkey, because otherwise nothing will go on at all. This is an attitude which we unfortunately had to adopt on the part of the Foreign Minister of Turkey in the Committee on Human Rights in this House, when it came to the question of whether the genocide on the Armenians has now been recognized as such for more than 80 years, that is, long before there was Turkey in the present form. You know, the treatment of this application has been postponed. It is in the drawers, but we hope that it will be put back on the agenda of the Human Rights Committee in the near future, of course provided that the government groups support it, but at the moment we do not have a signal for it. At the time, the Foreign Minister also said that it was necessary to negotiate with Turkey behind closed doors, in diplomatic talks, but there is no need to exert public pressure. I ask you, Mr Federal Minister, as you are here instead of the woman's foreign minister, do you really think that is the right way to put pressure on a country that wants to become the EU's candidate country? I am very clear here that we too were Greens in favour of Turkey's prospect of this accession status, as it needs talks, negotiations and contacts to make changes, so this is also quite clear from our side, but we are asking ourselves whether this attitude, which is only in the background and thereby put into the pressure that this government exerts on the people of the country, namely those who still call it standard terrorists, especially on people of Kurdish origin, many of whom sit in prisons and resist the prison conditions, is the right one. We wonder if it is the right attitude not to say something publicly about leaving time, hoping to wait until perhaps some change in the next few months and years. It's not for us! [[Congratulations with the Greens and representatives of the SPÖ.]] It is not only prisoners who put great hope on a public pressure, and also on the part of the EU, but also human rights organisations in Turkey, who also expect the EU to become active there and put pressure on the government. [[Congratulations with the Greens and representatives of the SPÖ.]] This is the reason why we have put this issue on the agenda today, because the lack of pressure from Austria and the European Union – and not from there too – is in our view a wrong procedure. There you must not wait, wait, hope, but you must express yourself clearly.

ParlaMint-AT_2001-05-11-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00070_d7e13107	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen of the House! I represent the Minister of Foreign Affairs, who is staying in Jordan on a visit, and I ask you to accept this. Mrs Lunacek! While you have correctly quoted, but only selectively, that Mrs Foreign Minister would have formulated such profound changes could not be put under time pressure if they were to be permanent, because very essential is the previous sentence, and this is what Mrs Foreign Minister says about deep-seated reform in Turkey in the legal field, the modernisation of Turkey, the strengthening of democracy and civil society, which is indeed things that cannot be done from today to tomorrow. In addition, I would like to refer to the opinion of the Foreign Minister, which I am now entitled to read. The Minister for Foreign Affairs, as appropriate, states: The commitment to respect for human rights in Turkey is a constant concern of Austrian foreign policy. The Federal Minister for Foreign Affairs therefore discussed the situation of human rights in a comprehensive manner during her last meeting with Foreign Minister Cem during the visit to Turkey, ladies and gentlemen. Foreign Minister Cem's detailed opinion is reflected in the written answer to your question. I am sure that on his forthcoming visit to Turkey in June, Federal Chancellor Schüssel will raise this issue again. On the human rights situation in Turkey, let me generally say the following: As a member of the European Union, Austria represents in principle the same position as the other members of the European Union on human rights in Turkey. Since the Helsinki Council in 1999, Turkey has been EU candidate on the basis of the same criteria that apply to the other candidate countries. The European Union supports the necessary reforms with a so-called pre-accession strategy, which has been concreteised, among other things, in an accession partnership. The Accession Partnership shall define both the political and economic criteria for the accession of the candidates. As part of the Accession Partnership, a permanent political dialogue will be held with Turkey. The main component of the accession partnership and political dialogue is the Copenhagen criteria. Their fulfilment is, of course, the prerequisite for accession negotiations with Turkey to take place at all. The Copenhagen criteria focus on human rights, as you know, ladies and gentlemen. Turkey is also aware of the need and the importance of reforms in terms of its wish for accession. This is reflected in the National Programme of Turkey on the adoption of the acquis, which was adopted on 19 November. It is the answer to the accession partnership and represents a concrete roadmap for the implementation of the reforms. This programme, as far as its text is already available in translation, is currently being examined by the EU Commission. On the human rights situation in concrete terms, it should be noted that since the candidate status has been recognised, on the one hand, a strong awareness of the Turkish government on the human rights issue, but on the other hand, there is no significant progress to be made. Indeed, an alarming development has occurred in prisons in relation to the starving prisoners. The current situation stems from a description of the Turkish Minister of Justice of the 8th century. May to EU ambassadors in Ankara, for example, and I would like to give you the following information: Currently, 571 prisoners participate in the hunger strikes. 168 of them are so-called death rowers in a total of 20 prisons. The strikers belong to several illegal organisations prohibited in Turkey. ==References====External links==* Official website ==References====External links== The Turkish Ministry of Justice's attempts to intervene through well-known Turkish writers as well as the Istanbul Bar Association against the strikers could not move them to give up. To date, 22 victims of death are to be complained of by prisoners and relatives. 167 prisoners are currently in hospitals, of which 86 accept treatment. It must be recognized that Turkey is constantly giving international observers the opportunity to take a look at the situation on the ground and to have talks with Turkish parliamentarians, government representatives and people active in the field of human rights. The Council of Europe's anti-torture committee, CPT in short, has repeatedly been in Turkey since December 2000, most recently in April this year. The report of the CPT on its last trip refers to the amendment of Article 16 anti-terrorism law. The text of the old article was unacceptable. The opportunity for social contacts now created is welcomed by the CPT. I can assure you, ladies and gentlemen, that Austria will not fail in its efforts to help secure human rights in Turkey. Thank you very much.

We have already said more often in the Committee and, of course, again and again on the basis of specific cases: Yes, we need to talk about whether this practice and the law are up-to-date or if there is a need for improvement. I am glad that at least this occasion is now leading to this general debate and possibly also to changes. I add another point to the needs mentioned by my previous speakers of rethinking protection in connection with intimidation attempts at civil law. I believe that it is too late for a potentially damaged person or a injured person – that is, a private person who is affected by claims of a publicly emphasised personality in their reputation, professional progress or creditworthiness – if, after a number of years, it may result in a court judgment giving its right. In other words, I believe that there is also a need for a better protection mechanism for people affected by public claims by celebrities in their progress. But now to the specific case: I also voted against extradition in the committee – and I will now do so here in plenary – as always, because I believe that we have a practice and a legal situation to establish in general and separate from the individual case, and because I also believe that immunity – especially as a protection against economically powerful lobbys – will have and will have significance. We should act in this direction, and I am actually not prepared to give up today's existing immunity, perhaps there and there, before we have at least the contours a new regime. In the specific case, I would like to point out the following. This is about § 301 of the Criminal Code, and it is about the really unbearable situation that, as we found in the Committee, for political reasons, a Member of this House draws attention to the fact that, not a private person, but a political representative – a high-level political representative, a liberal MP to the Lower Austrian Parliament – has committed certain actions that have been disputed in the public. That is to say, it is about the debate between politicians and politicians as well as whether or not a behavior was right. Here a truthful claim is made to the public, and journalists also speak truthfully about it. But then the strange case occurs that this House does not doubt: Yes, the honourable Member, who has brought this issue to the public, has acted for political reasons. – It may, however, be that the others who have reported this incident are interested, namely that in October last year, the Member of Parliament, Pilz, quoted in his capacity as executive officer, a disciplinary knowledge of a member of the state of Lower Austria. This knowledge becomes a criminal case for journalists who report on it, with nothing exactly known about the persecutions that have already been committed, namely against journalists who have, in truth, reported on an incident that this House has unanimously established by the committee responsible that this is in a political context. [[Abg. Auer: Disciplinary knowledge...!]] These journalists are becoming now – you don't know exactly: as witnesses, as accused? – entered into a procedure, although, as said, they have truthfully reported on a political causa. That is where I put you... – Yes, a disciplinary knowledge that corresponds to the facts and that concerns a person, the political representative, and that is a high-ranking political representative! [[Abg. Auer: A Disciplinary Knowledge!]] These are incidents that we have often discussed in this House, namely illegal ECIS questions. [[Call the ÖVP: This is a boulevard story!]] This is not a boulevard story, but it is a question of data protection, the rights of citizens, freedom of the media and fundamental rights. This is not a boulevard, ladies and gentlemen! [[Congratulation with the Greens and the SPÖ.]] It really raises the question – and I think that this is a question that concerns the Presidential in its entirety and the House as a whole – whether, when a very strong political debate on fundamental rights, fundamental freedoms and freedom of the media takes place here, some people who have done nothing but exercise their profession – and this includes the fundamental right of reporting, freedom of the media – will find themselves in court for it at once, in an unexplained function. There have already been persecutions. Ladies and gentlemen! All this immeasurableness makes it highly understandable to me personally that Member of the Pilz says: It is almost unbearable for me personally that those people who have reported about my press conference now face persecution while this House finds that I, i.e. fungus, have acted in a political context and therefore I am not extradited. This is a contradiction that is unsolvable – except for the rapid correction of the § 301 Criminal Code in the sense of how we suggested this yesterday with our urgent request, and by generally discussing, not in relation to the individual case, but in general, a modern right of immunity and coming to an appropriate decision.

ParlaMint-AT_2001-05-11-021-XXI-NRSITZ-00070_d7e14071	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! I have a slightly different access to these immunities and to the practice of dealing with this immunity in connection with alleged criminal acts of politicians. First of all, I should like to point out that I am in favour of the full immunity for all that the politicians here in this House do or say. There is no question at all that the interest in the investigation of scandals – even if some accusation is wrong – must be higher than other legal goods in this context. In the present case, however, we are dealing with a very different question, namely whether statements by Members who have fallen outside Parliament, for example in press conferences, are subject to a different appreciation than statements by, so to speak, ordinary politicians: from politicians without immunity, for example at community level. I can't see any reason for different handling. I can also justify this: If today a politician makes serious accusations in a press conference outside of the house, according to the constant saying practice of the Austrian courts, according to § 1330 ABGB, he can be used for credit damages, he can also be used for damages. All efforts to reduce the court's attorney's rates and fees do not alter the risk of the individual politician, who makes certain credit-damaging statements outside this House, to be subject to judicial prosecution. Such a persecution may well also be that it is used for damages, even in millions. – This is constant practice, and that is applicable law. In my opinion, it is therefore entirely inhomogeneous to allow such a practice to be used by politicians under civil law – and I also believe that if they are right to rob someone of their credit, to bring about a massive asset-law disadvantage – while being a representative, when one makes such a statement outside Parliament, is under the cover of immunity and cannot be held responsible for criminal law. What is the difference that would justify this? For all of us who are in the same situation – even if we do not every two days in the "Landtmann" announce the scandal of the decade as colleague Pilz – it would have to be much more unpleasant to use civil law, perhaps also with complaints in millions, than because of a possible offense according to § 301 StGB. There is an inhomogeneity, and that is why I am in favour of in this case we agree with the extradition of Mr Pilz – but this is also for another reason. Mr Pilz asked for extradition in his speech yesterday. Although he has not enforced himself in his own party, he has worked hard to this end: Give me the opportunity to prove my guilt in court! I will personally comply with this request, because there are two possibilities and explanations: Firstly, that Mr. Pilz did not mean seriously that he had, so to speak, made a mental reservation, so he could have been hypocritical and asked, "Deliver me!" – in the consciousness or in the certainty that it will not be delivered anyway. But then he earned an extradition. Or, in the other case, if it was actually serious to him – and for that it speaks a lot – then it should not be so that Mr. Pilz makes a mistake here. At least I will comply with this request and agree to an extradition. [[Congratulations on Members of the Liberals.]] I can also bring another point of view, because it is always the question of what a threat Mr Pilz poses. First of all, I would like to say that I would not at all mix the discussion about the criminal responsibility of journalists and the criminal responsibility of Mr Pilz, which is perfectly clear to me. We know that this is the presumption of innocence. I believe, of course, that the presumption of innocence applies to journalists, because if a journalist is invited to a press conference today, at which something is revealed, and he then writes about it, it is no question at all that it can never be a intentional offence of a journalist. So please: The attempt of my colleague Pilz to put himself in a boat with the journalists here, so to speak, to "invite" journalists to his discharge, because they were also threatened by criminal law, lacks any basis. The journalist, who has written truthfully about the content of this press conference, has, of course, acted bona fide, and I have no doubt that, if such a procedure is started at all, it has to end with an immediate attitude. However, I dare to doubt whether this also applies to Mr Pilz, because Mr Pilz can be expected to know the relevant provisions of the Criminal Code, especially when it comes to the disclosure of secrets. [[Abg. Dr. Herbs: What about Haider?]] My colleague, Mr Graf, has already rightly pointed out that it is not just a matter of trying to suggest, as Mr Petrovic has suggested, that there should be a conflict at the same level – politicians against politicians. In this case, I would actually be able to gain a lot of views, but it has also been revealed that there is a knowledge of discipline that concerns a non-political person. [[Abg. Dr. Kostelka: Talk time!]] In this respect, I do not share the view of the constitutional lawyer Mayer, who believes that, in this case, one has to consider between the protection of secrecy on the one hand and the right to freedom of expression on the other, and that this consideration is in favour of the right to freedom of expression, because it is an old tradition – and that is not only so stuck in civil service law – that disciplinary proceedings are negotiated without the exclusion of the public. It is something else whether a person has initiated a disciplinary procedure or whether he is being prosecuted, which is then negotiated in a public main proceedings. For example, this is also true of pharmacists: If a pharmacist is involved in a disciplinary procedure, it will be excluded from the public; it is also true of lawyers. There is the need for the individual, the person concerned, not to be pulled through the cocoa in the public. That means: There may well be a legally sound distinction between reporting on a criminal-public procedure and a procedure involving disciplinary offences, which appear in a much milder light than those relating to criminal acts which may be committed on their own initiative. Even if one reads through this disciplinary knowledge, which the Green Group has distributed, one can see that again and again completely innocent people are being involved, for example, someone has been heard because of the friend, or what else I know. It is certainly not pleasant for those concerned to find themselves in the newspapers. . I therefore believe that there are very good reasons why the content of internal disciplinary procedures is not disclosed to the public. These provisions should actually be known by a representative like Mr Pilz, and if he knows them, he is also responsible. I would also like to add: This section 301 StGB is not a section, which was published on 4 April. He was suddenly put into force in 2001 to pursue misguided opposition politicians, but this is a provision which the then Minister of Justice Broda introduced by a government template in 1975 and which is quite clear from the scene. Whether Mr Pilz will be able to benefit from the culprit's reasons would be seen if he were to be extradited. Mr Pilz! As exit strategy, I would suggest the following, as it seems that you will not be delivered: Are you really so serious about your rehabilitation in court? – On behalf of the Republic, he wants to be acquitted. – You are so rarely here in the high house, you were there yesterday just to the urgent, you have now found yourself "kindly" because it is about your extradition. Otherwise you'll never be seen here! They are much more often in the "Landtmann" at press conferences than here in the House. I therefore think it would be reasonable for you to spend two or three months of your life outside the house as non-deputies and to devote yourself to the rehabilitation of your person before the criminal court.
